{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-10.89052pt}{5.89098pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,,\qquad\varepsilon_{i}\in\{\pm\}.

For brevity we denote this last stated tangle by
{}_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\,\alpha
and
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}=v_{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\varepsilon_{n}}
. Note that in this figure there are two implicit sums,
\alpha
is a linear combination of tangles and an element of
V^{\otimes n}
is a linear combination of
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}
’s. They will remain implicit in the following.

In the context of stated skein algebras one needs the boundary points of the tangle
\alpha
to be above the boundary arc though they are on the bottom at its right in the context of morphisms in
{\rm Sk}_{{\rm TL}}(\Sigma)
. One wants to simply push the boundary points through the boundary edge and up above it, but without braiding the strands with one another. Therefore we use a global automorphism of
\Sigma\times[0,1]
. Consider an isotopy of the identity on
\Sigma\times[0,1]
which is trivial far from the corner and in it pushes the bottom boundary through and up above the edge. It results in the global automorphism
\psi_{hv}
of
\Sigma\times[0,1]
which maps a tangle “from skein categories” to one “from stated skein algebras” and preserves the order as desired, namely the bottom points on the leftmost will end up above. We will still denote the modified tangle
\psi_{hv}(\alpha)
by
\alpha
. Now, it only needs states to give an element of
\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
.

We set

\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha):=\begin{cases}V^{\otimes n}\to\mathscr{S}(\Sigma),\\
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\mapsto{}_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\,\alpha,\end{cases}

so where
{}_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\,\alpha
is the tangle
\psi_{hv}(\alpha)
with states
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
from top to bottom. It is well defined because the tangle representing
\alpha
is well defined up to isotopy and Kauffman-bracket relations, which are quotiented out in
\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Given a tangle
\alpha
, the map
\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha)\colon V^{\otimes n}\to\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
is an
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodule morphism.

Proof.

Note that we still see
\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
as a right
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodule even though we draw the edge at the left. Its comodule structure is given by
\Delta(_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\,\alpha)=\sum_{\vec{\eta}\in\{\pm\}^{n}}{}_{\vec{\eta}}\alpha\otimes{}_{\vec{\eta}}\beta_{\vec{\varepsilon}}
, and here
{}_{\vec{\eta}}\beta_{\vec{\varepsilon}}
is a product
\prod_{i}{}_{\eta_{i}}\beta_{\varepsilon_{i}}
in
\mathscr{S}(B)
. The comodule structure on
V^{\otimes n}
is given by
\Delta(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}})=\sum_{\vec{\eta}}v_{\vec{\eta}}\otimes\prod_{i}x_{\eta_{i},\varepsilon_{i}}
, where
x_{\eta,\varepsilon}
is the
v_{\eta}
part of
\Delta(v_{\varepsilon})
. Namely,
x_{+,+}=a
,
x_{+,-}=b
,
x_{-,+}=c
and
x_{-,-}=d
. Under the isomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\simeq\mathscr{S}(B)
,
x_{\eta,\varepsilon}\mapsto{}_{\eta}\beta_{\varepsilon}
.
Thus
\Delta(\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha)(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}))=\Delta(_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\,\alpha)=\sum_{\vec{\eta}}\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha)(v_{\vec{\eta}})\otimes{}_{\vec{\eta}}\beta_{\vec{\varepsilon}}=(\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha)\otimes{\rm Id}_{\mathscr{S}(B)})(\Delta(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}))
.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

The maps
\operatorname{St}_{W}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{{\rm TL}}(\Sigma)}(W\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}}(W,\mathscr{S}(\Sigma))
,
W\in{\rm TL}
, define a natural transformation
\operatorname{St}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{{\rm TL}}(\Sigma)}(-\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)\Rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(-,\mathscr{S}(\Sigma))
between functors
{\rm TL}^{\rm op}\to{\rm Vect}_{k}
.

Proof.

For
g\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm TL}}(V^{\otimes n},V^{\otimes m})
,
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{{\rm TL}}(\Sigma)}(V^{\otimes m}\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)
and
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\in V^{\otimes n}

, one needs to check that
\operatorname{St}_{V^{\otimes m}}(\alpha)(g(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}))=\operatorname{St}_{V^{\otimes n}}(\alpha\circ(g\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}})
. Morphisms of
{\rm TL}
are generated under composition and juxtaposition by identities, caps and cups, so one only needs to prove the result for these last two.
We will need some explicit computations of these caps and cups. Recall from Remark
2.21
, or
[

, Theorem 4.2]
, that to do so one uses the isomorphism

of Definition

and sets

{}^{\mu}_{\nu}\reflectbox{$C$}
from the left boundary skein relations of Proposition
2.27
coincides with
\cap(v_{\mu}\otimes v_{\nu})
and indeed
\cap(v_{+}\otimes v_{+})={\rm ev}\big({-}q^{\frac{5}{2}}v_{-}^{*}\otimes v_{+}\big)=0={}^{+}_{+}\reflectbox{$C$}
,
\cap(v_{-}\otimes v_{-})={\rm ev}\big(q^{\frac{1}{2}}v_{+}^{*}\otimes v_{-}\big)=0={}^{-}_{-}\reflectbox{$C$}
,
\cap(v_{+}\otimes v_{-})={\rm ev}\big({-}q^{\frac{5}{2}}v_{-}^{*}\otimes v_{-}\big)=-q^{\frac{5}{2}}={}^{+}_{-}\reflectbox{$C$}
and
\cap(v_{-}\otimes v_{+})={\rm ev}\big(q^{\frac{1}{2}}v_{+}^{*}\otimes v_{+}\big)=q^{\frac{1}{2}}={}^{-}_{+}\reflectbox{$C$}
.

Now let
g=\

,
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{{\rm TL}}(\Sigma)}\big(V^{\otimes n+2}\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing\big)
and
v=v_{\varepsilon_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\varepsilon_{n}}\in V^{\otimes n}
. One can directly compute
\cup(1)=\big({\rm Id}_{V}\otimes\varphi^{-1}\big)\circ\mathop{\rm coev}(1)=\big({\rm Id}_{V}\otimes\varphi^{-1}\big)(v_{-}\otimes v_{-}^{*}+v_{+}\otimes v_{+}^{*})=-q^{-\frac{5}{2}}v_{-}\otimes v_{+}+q^{-\frac{1}{2}}v_{+}\otimes v_{-}
.

Recall from Proposition
2.42
and the discussion below that the internal endomorphism object of
\varnothing
with respect to the
\mathcal{E}
-action is an object
X
equipped with an isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\rm TL}(\Sigma)}(-\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)\allowbreak\tilde{\Rightarrow}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(-,X)
in
[{\rm TL}^{\rm op},{\rm Vect}_{k}]

Theorem 3.4
.

The natural transformation
\operatorname{St}
is a natural isomorphism and exhibits
\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
as the internal endomorphism object of the empty set. Namely one can take
A_{\Sigma}=\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
as
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodule.

Proof.

We exhibit an inverse to
\operatorname{St}
. Let
W=V^{\otimes n}\in{\rm TL}
, which decomposes as a direct sum of simples
W_{i}
. Let
f\colon W\to\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
be a morphism in
\mathcal{E}
, and denote by
f_{i}
its restriction on
W_{i}
. We want a morphism
\operatorname{St}_{W}^{-1}(f)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{{\rm TL}}(\Sigma)}(W\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)
, which is equivalent to a collection of morphisms
{\rm St}_{W}^{-1}(f_{i})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)}(W_{i}\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)
. Each
f_{i}
is determined by its value on a single element
w_{i}\in W_{i}
(pick a highest weight element for example), it extends to all
W_{i}
by applying the
\mathcal{U}_{q^{2}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-action (recall from Proposition
2.9
that
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodules correspond to
\mathcal{U}_{q^{2}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-modules). Choose any stated tangle
a_{i}
representing the element
f_{i}(w_{i})\in\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
. Denote by
\alpha_{i}
its underlying tangle,
n_{i}=\left|\partial\alpha_{i}\right|
its number of boundary points and
\vec{\varepsilon}_{i}
its states. This representative is well defined up to the boundary skein relations, the usual skein relations and isotopy. The assignment
w_{i}\mapsto v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i}}
extends to a unique
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-morphism
g_{i}\colon W_{i}\to V^{\otimes n_{i}}
by applying the
\mathcal{U}_{q^{2}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
-action. We then set
\operatorname{St}_{W}^{-1}(f_{i})=\alpha_{i}\circ(g_{i}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})
. Note that here
\alpha_{i}
denotes the tangle
\alpha_{i}
seen as a morphism in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)
, so we actually mean
\psi_{hv}^{-1}(\alpha_{i})
, the same tangle but with boundary points at the bottom instead of the left. We have to check that this definition does not depend on the representative
a_{i}
. Usual skein relations and isotopy do not change
\alpha_{i}
seen as a morphism in the skein category. The boundary skein relations are equivalent to naturality using Proposition
3.3
. Namely, another representative
a^{\prime}_{i}
has to be of the form
\alpha^{\prime}_{i}=\alpha_{i}\circ(g\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})
, for some
g\in{\rm TL}
, with states
{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i}}^{\ {}^{\prime}}
such that
g({\vec{\varepsilon}_{i}}^{\ {}^{\prime}})=\vec{\varepsilon}_{i}
. Therefore the
g^{\prime}_{i}
for
a^{\prime}_{i}
will be such that
g_{i}=g\circ g^{\prime}_{i}
. Then we simply check that
\alpha_{i}\circ(g_{i}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})=\alpha_{i}\circ(g\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})\circ(g^{\prime}_{i}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})=\alpha^{\prime}_{i}\circ(g^{\prime}_{i}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})
, and
{\rm St}_{W}^{-1}(f_{i})
is well defined.

For simplicity we assume that
\operatorname{St}_{W}^{-1}(f_{i})
and
g_{i}
are actually defined on all
W
but are 0 except on
W_{i}
, namely we precompose by the projection
W\twoheadrightarrow W_{i}
, and thus we set
\operatorname{St}_{W}^{-1}(f)=\sum_{i}\operatorname{St}_{W}^{-1}(f_{i})
.

It is now easy to check that
\operatorname{St}_{W}^{-1}
is the inverse of
\operatorname{St}_{W}
. For
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\in W
, suppose
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\in W_{i_{0}}\subseteq W
lies in a simple, or decompose it in the
W_{i}
’s, and write
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}=X\cdot w_{i_{0}}
,
X\in\mathcal{U}_{q^{2}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
. One has
g_{i_{0}}(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}})=X\cdot v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i_{0}}}
and for
j\neq{i_{0}},\ g_{j}(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}})=0
. Thus:

\displaystyle\operatorname{St}_{W}\big(\operatorname{St}_{W}^{-1}(f)\big)(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{St}_{W}\bigg(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\circ(g_{i}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})\bigg)(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}})=\sum_{i}\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha_{i}\circ(g_{i}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}})

\displaystyle\overset{{\rm Proposition~\ref{propStNat}}}{=}\sum_{i}\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha_{i})(g_{i}(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}))=\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha_{i_{0}})(X\cdot v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i_{0}}})

\displaystyle\overset{{\rm Proposition~\ref{propStOqmorph}}}{=}X\cdot\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha_{i_{0}})(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i_{0}}})=X\cdot a_{i_{0}}=X\cdot f_{i_{0}}(w_{i_{0}})

\displaystyle=f_{i_{0}}(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}})=f(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}).

Symmetrically, let
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{{\rm TL}}(\Sigma)}(W\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)
and
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\in W
. Set
f=\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha)\colon V^{\otimes\left|\partial\alpha\right|}\to\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
, in the definition of
\operatorname{St}_{W}^{-1}(f)
one has
\alpha_{i}=\alpha
and
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i}}=w_{i}
, so
g_{i}
is the inclusion
W_{i}\hookrightarrow W
. If
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\in W_{i}\subseteq W
, one has
\operatorname{St}_{W}^{-1}(\operatorname{St}_{W}(\alpha))=\sum_{i}\alpha\circ(g_{i}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})=\alpha\circ({\rm Id}_{W}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})=\alpha
.
∎

Proposition 3.5
.

The algebra structure inherited from the internal endomorphism object structure on
\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
coincides with its usual algebra structure. Namely
A_{\Sigma}=\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
as
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodule algebras.

Proof.

Recall that the product on
\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
is given by stacking the left tangle
a
above the right one
b
, and the product on
A_{\Sigma}
is defined by evaluation and composition maps on internal
\operatorname{Hom}
objects, in Definition
2.39
. Graphically, the stated tangles
a
and
b
, which we see as morphisms
\alpha
and
\beta
in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)
with prescribed inputs
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}
and
v_{\vec{\eta}}
, map to the morphism
\alpha\circ({\rm Id}_{V^{\otimes\left|\partial\alpha\right|}}\rhd\beta)
in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)
with prescribed input
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\otimes v_{\vec{\eta}}
, which we see as the stated tangle
a
above
b
:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\ v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\otimes v_{\vec{\eta}}.\end{array}

The evaluation map
{\rm ev}_{\varnothing,\varnothing}\colon\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)\rhd\varnothing\to\varnothing
is the image under
\operatorname{St}^{-1}
of
{\rm Id}_{\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)}
. We have not constructed
\operatorname{St}^{-1}
on all
\mathcal{E}
above, but only on
{\rm TL}
, and it extends by cocontinuity in Proposition
2.42
. The comodule
\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
decomposes as simples as
\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}^{+}}\mathcal{U}_{q^{2}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\cdot\alpha
where
\mathcal{B}^{+}
is the set of
\mathfrak{o}
-ordered simple stated tangles with only + states, see
[
7
, Theorem 4.6(b)]
. These stated tangles with only + states are simply a way to represent canonically a tangle without state information, and again in the following we will see
\alpha
as a morphism in
\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)}\big(V^{\otimes\left|\partial\alpha\right|},\varnothing\big)
, which is actually
\psi_{hv}^{-1}(\alpha)
.

We denote by
W_{\alpha}=\mathcal{U}_{q^{2}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\cdot\alpha
and
g_{\alpha}\colon W_{\alpha}\to V^{\otimes\left|\partial\alpha\right|}
the inclusion mapping
\alpha
to its states
v_{\overrightarrow{+\cdots+}}
. Then:

{\rm ev}_{\varnothing,\varnothing}=\operatorname{St}^{-1}({\rm Id}_{\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)})=\oplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}^{+}}\operatorname{St}^{-1}(W_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{S}(\Sigma))=\oplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}^{+}}\alpha\circ(g_{\alpha}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}).

The composition map
c\colon\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)\to\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
is the image under
St
of the morphism
{\rm ev}_{\varnothing,\varnothing}\circ({\rm Id}_{\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)}\rhd{\rm ev}_{\varnothing,\varnothing})\colon(\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathscr{S}(\Sigma))\rhd\varnothing\to\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)\rhd\varnothing\to\varnothing
. This morphism is the double sum:

\displaystyle\oplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}^{+}}\oplus_{\beta\in\mathcal{B}^{+}}(\alpha\circ(g_{\alpha}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))\circ({\rm Id}_{\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)}\rhd(\beta\circ(g_{\beta}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})))

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\oplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}^{+}}\oplus_{\beta\in\mathcal{B}^{+}}\alpha\circ({\rm Id}_{V^{\otimes\left|\partial\alpha\right|}}\rhd\beta)\circ(g_{\alpha}\rhd g_{\beta}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}).

The product is obtained by applying
\operatorname{St}
to this morphism. For
a,b\in\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
, write
a=X\cdot\alpha
and
b=Y\cdot\beta
with
X,Y\in\mathcal{U}_{q^{2}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})
and
\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal{B}^{+}
. Thus
a
has states
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}=X\cdot v_{\overrightarrow{+\cdots+}}
and
b
has states
v_{\vec{\eta}}=Y\cdot v_{\overrightarrow{+\cdots+}}
. By naturality:

\displaystyle c(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{St}_{\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)}({\rm ev}_{\varnothing,\varnothing}\circ({\rm Id}_{\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)}\rhd{\rm ev}_{\varnothing,\varnothing}))(a\otimes b)

\displaystyle=\oplus_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}}\oplus_{\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{+}}\operatorname{St}(\alpha^{\prime}\circ({\rm Id}_{V^{\otimes\left|\partial\alpha^{\prime}\right|}}\rhd\beta^{\prime}))((g_{\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes g_{\beta^{\prime}})(a\otimes b))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{St}(\alpha\circ({\rm Id}_{V^{\otimes\left|\partial\alpha\right|}}\rhd\beta))((g_{\alpha}\otimes g_{\beta})(a\otimes b))=\operatorname{St}(\alpha\circ({\rm Id}_{V^{\otimes\left|\partial\alpha\right|}}\rhd\beta))(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}}\otimes v_{\vec{\eta}})

which is precisely the usual product of
a
and
b
in
\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)
.
∎

4
Multi-edges

We define internal skein algebras for surfaces with more than one boundary, and possibly left or right boundary edges. We show they are isomorphic to stated skein algebras when
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}
, and re-prove their excision properties using excision properties of skein categories.

4.1
Right internal skein algebras

In order to extend the definition of internal skein algebras to surfaces with multiple boundary edges, we would need a notion of left and right action to be able to glue surfaces together, such that internal skein algebras satisfy excision properties, just like stated skein algebras. One subtlety though is that one is only allowed to talk about right
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodules in the context of internal skein algebras, to stay in the category
\mathcal{E}
(as opposed to the stated skein algebra context).

Definition 4.1

(
[
6
, Section 3.2]
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be a surface with a chosen boundary interval, which we see at the right of the surface. One can make a construction similar to Definition
2.44
to have a right action functor
\lhd\colon{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)\otimes{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)\to{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)
. It differs the one
\rhd
of Definition
2.44
only by rotating the disk by 180 degrees.

The right moduli algebra
A_{\Sigma}^{R}
of
[
2
, Section 5.2]
, or right internal skein algebra of
[
11
]
, is the internal endomorphism algebra of the empty set in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)
with respect to this
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)^{\otimes\text{-}{\rm op}}
-module structure.

\sqcup

Definition 4.2
.

Denote by
rot
the diffeomorphism of the disk given by
180^{\circ}
rotation. By Remark
2.16
it induces an automorphism
(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}:={\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}({\rm rot})\colon{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)\to{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
which squares to the identity. For
X\in{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
, we call
X^{\operatorname{ht}}:={\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}({\rm rot})(X)
the half-twisted
X
. One easily checks that
(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}
is anti-monoidal, namely
(X\otimes Y)^{\operatorname{ht}}=Y^{\operatorname{ht}}\otimes X^{\operatorname{ht}}
, because
rot
reverses left-right order.
The diffeomorphism
rot
is isotopic to the identity by rotating from 0 to
180^{\circ}
. This isotopy induces a natural isomorphism
{\operatorname{ht}}\colon{\rm Id}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\tilde{\Rightarrow}(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}
called the half twist, which squares to the twist (the
360^{\circ}
rotation).

Remember from Remark
2.16
that
{\operatorname{ht}}
is given on
n
blackboard framed points on the real axis by
n
parallelly half-twisted vertical strands, namely drawn on the half twisted ribbon
. Naturality, namely
{\operatorname{ht}}_{W}\circ f\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}^{-1}=f^{\operatorname{ht}}
, expresses the fact that one can untwist a top half twist and a bottom anti-half-twist by half twisting the middle. For
f={\operatorname{ht}}_{V}
one gets
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V^{\operatorname{ht}}}={\operatorname{ht}}_{V}^{\operatorname{ht}}
. Note too that

{\operatorname{ht}}_{V\otimes W}=({\operatorname{ht}}_{W}\otimes{\operatorname{ht}}_{V})\circ c_{V,W}\qquad\text{by}\quad\hbox to29.25pt{\vbox to29.25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.62637pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

Now, one simply has
\rhd=\lhd\circ\operatorname{fl}\circ((-)^{\operatorname{ht}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)})
and
\lhd\circ\operatorname{fl}=\rhd\circ((-)^{\operatorname{ht}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)})
, where
\operatorname{fl}
is the flip of tensors. And indeed a left action turns into a right action under an anti-monoidal functor. Moreover, the natural isomorphism
{\operatorname{ht}}\colon{\rm Id}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\tilde{\Rightarrow}(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}
gives a natural isomorphism
{\operatorname{ht}}\rhd-:=\rhd\circ({\operatorname{ht}}\otimes{\rm Id}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)})\colon\rhd\tilde{\Rightarrow}\lhd\circ\operatorname{fl}
.

Remark 4.3
.

One can give an explicit relation between left and right internal skein algebras. Internal skein algebras are only defined up to isomorphism, so this description is just one choice. Actually, we will give another one below. Consider the internal skein algebra
A_{\Sigma}
defined as in Section
2.4
by seeing the red arc at the left, with a natural isomorphism
\sigma\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)}(-\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)\tilde{\Rightarrow}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(-,A_{\Sigma})
. Then one has natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)}(\varnothing\lhd V,\varnothing)=\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)}\!\big(V^{\operatorname{ht}}\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing\big)\overset{\sigma_{V^{\operatorname{ht}}}}{\to}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}\!\big(V^{\operatorname{ht}},A_{\Sigma}\big)\overset{(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}}{\to}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}\!\big(V,A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}\big).

Namely,
A_{\Sigma}^{R}\simeq A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}
as object of
{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
, but with natural isomorphism
(\sigma_{(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}})^{\operatorname{ht}}
.

Remark 4.4
.

Note that the half twist is defined on
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
and not on
\mathcal{V}
, which is its full subcategory of objects with only one coloured point, with blackboard framing. The half twist will map such an object to a point with anti-blackboard framing, so it does not stabilise
\mathcal{V}
. The equivalence of categories
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)\simeq\mathcal{V}
preserves properties of the half twist only up to natural isomorphism, and depends on the choice of a quasi-inverse of the inclusion. The one described in Remark
2.12
will map the half twist on
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
to the identity on
\mathcal{V}
, but if we had chosen to restore the framing counter-clockwise it would map it to the full twist. In
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}
an actual half twist exists, see
[
24
]
, and we will study it below. However, in general we will prefer a construction that uses the half twist only on the disk inserted in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)
, where it is well defined, and not on
\mathcal{V}
. In particular, the above description
A_{\Sigma}^{R}\simeq A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}
holds in
{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
but has an unclear meaning in
\mathcal{E}
(it depends on the choice of some quasi-inverses).

Definition 4.5
.

There is another explicit relation between left and right internal skein algebras using
{\operatorname{ht}}\rhd-
to relate the right action to the left one. Set
\sigma^{R}=\sigma\circ({\operatorname{ht}}\rhd\varnothing)\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)}(\varnothing\lhd-,\varnothing)\allowbreak\tilde{\Rightarrow}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(-,A_{\Sigma})
. What we mean is that for
V\in\mathcal{V}
and
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)}(\varnothing\lhd V,\varnothing)
, we have
\alpha\circ\allowbreak({\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)}(V\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)
, and
\sigma^{R}_{V}(\alpha):=\sigma_{V}(\alpha\circ({\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))
.

Proposition 4.6
.

The natural isomorphism
\sigma^{R}
exhibits
A_{\Sigma}
as the right internal skein algebra. The algebra structure
m^{R}\colon A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}\to A_{\Sigma}
inherited from this right internal endomorphism object structure differs from the left one
m\colon A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}\to A_{\Sigma}
by a braiding:
m^{R}=m\circ c_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}
.

Namely, the right internal skein algebras introduced in
[
11
]
and
[
2
]
are the braided opposites of the left ones introduced in Section
2.4
.

Proof.

The
\sigma^{R}_{V}
’s form a natural isomorphism: let
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{V}}(V,W)
and
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)}(\varnothing\lhd W,\varnothing)
, one checks:

\displaystyle\sigma_{V}^{R}(\alpha\circ({\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\lhd f))

\displaystyle=\sigma_{V}^{R}\big(\alpha\circ\big(f^{\operatorname{ht}}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\big)\big):=\sigma_{V}\big(\alpha\circ\big(f^{\operatorname{ht}}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\big)\circ({\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{V}(\alpha\circ({\operatorname{ht}}_{W}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})\circ(f\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))=\sigma_{W}^{R}(\alpha)\circ f

by using naturality of
\sigma
and of
{\operatorname{ht}}\rhd-
. For
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(V,A_{\Sigma})
one has
\big(\sigma^{R}_{V}\big)^{-1}(f)=\sigma_{V}^{-1}(f)\circ({\operatorname{ht}}_{V}^{-1}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})
.
Therefore
\big(A_{\Sigma},\sigma^{R}\big)
is the internal endomorphism object of the empty set in
{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)
with respect to the right
{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
-action by
Proposition
2.42
. We now study its algebra structure.
The evaluation map is

\displaystyle{\rm ev}^{R}:

\displaystyle=\big(\sigma^{R}_{A_{\Sigma}}\big)^{-1}({\rm Id}_{A_{\Sigma}})=\sigma_{A_{\Sigma}}^{-1}({\rm Id}_{A_{\Sigma}})\circ\big({\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}}^{-1}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\big)

\displaystyle={\rm ev}\circ\big({\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}}^{-1}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\big)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}(\Sigma)}(\varnothing\lhd A_{\Sigma},\varnothing).

The product, or composition map, is:

\displaystyle m^{R}:

\displaystyle=\sigma^{R}_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}\big({\rm ev}^{R}\circ\big({\rm ev}^{R}\lhd{\rm Id}_{A_{\Sigma}}\big)\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}\big({\rm ev}\circ\big({\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}}^{-1}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\big)\circ{\rm Id}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}\rhd\big({\rm ev}\circ\big({\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}}^{-1}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\big)\big)\circ({\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}\big({\rm ev}\circ({\rm Id}_{A_{\Sigma}}\rhd{\rm ev})\circ\big({\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}}^{-1}\otimes{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}}^{-1}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\big)\circ({\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})\big)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}({\rm ev}\circ({\rm Id}_{A_{\Sigma}}\rhd{\rm ev})\circ(c_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))=m\circ c_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}.

The units
\sigma^{R}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}}({\rm Id}_{\varnothing}):=\sigma_{1_{\mathcal{V}}}({\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\circ({\operatorname{ht}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))=\sigma_{1_{\mathcal{V}}}({\rm Id}_{\varnothing})
coincide.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

In
{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
, one has
\sigma^{R}_{V}(\alpha):=\sigma_{V}(\alpha\circ({\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\rhd{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))=\sigma_{V^{\operatorname{ht}}}(\alpha)\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}
. If one post-composes with
{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}
this is exactly
(\sigma_{V^{\operatorname{ht}}})^{\operatorname{ht}}
. Hence the two descriptions of right internal skein algebras we gave,
\big(A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}},{\sigma_{(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}}}^{\operatorname{ht}}\big)
in Remark
4.3
and
(A_{\Sigma},\sigma\circ({\operatorname{ht}}\rhd-))
in Proposition
4.6
, are isomorphic (as they should) by
{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}\colon A_{\Sigma}\to A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}
. The product on
A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}
is given by

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}\circ m^{R}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}\otimes{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}

\displaystyle={\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}\circ m\circ c_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}\otimes{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}

\displaystyle={\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}\circ m\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}=m^{\operatorname{ht}}.

4.2
Multi-edges internal skein algebras

We extend the definition of internal skein algebras to the multi-edge context, and define them as internal endomorphism algebras of the empty set in the skein category with multiple boundary actions, as expected. We check that they still describe skein categories well-enough.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a marked surface with
n
boundary edges labelled either as left (numbered 1 to
k
) or as right (numbered
k+1
to
n
) edges. Each left (resp. right) boundary edge induces a left
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
-action (resp. left
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)^{\otimes\text{-}{\rm op}}
-action) on
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
, which all commute, so one has a left
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)^{\otimes k}\otimes\big({\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)^{\otimes\text{-}{\rm op}}\big)^{\otimes n-k}
-action
\bm{\triangleright}
on
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
. We denote its components by
\rhd_{i}
or
\lhd_{i}\circ\operatorname{fl}\colon{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)\otimes{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})\to{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
,
1\leq i\leq n
, though we forget the indices when they are understood. When there are missing components they will be implicitly filled by
1_{\mathcal{V}}
. We may also write
(V_{1},\dots,V_{k}){\rhd}\varnothing\lhd(V_{k+1},\dots,V_{n})
instead of
(V_{1},\dots,V_{n})\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing
.

The internal skein algebra
A_{\mathfrak{S}}
is the internal endomorphism object of the empty set in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
with respect to the
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)^{\otimes k}\otimes({\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)^{\otimes\text{-}{\rm op}})^{\otimes n-k}
-action. It is an algebra object in
\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}\simeq\operatorname{Free}\big(\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n}\big)\simeq\operatorname{Free}\big({\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)^{\otimes n}\big)
, where
\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}
has opposite tensor products on the last
n-k
components. We denote this monoidal structure by
\bm{\otimes}
. We denote by
\otimes_{i}
the tensor product on coordinate
i
, and adopt the same convention as with
\bm{\triangleright}
filling with missing
1_{\mathcal{V}}
’s and eventually writing
(V_{1},\dots,V_{k})\otimes W\otimes(V_{k+1},\dots,V_{n})
instead of
(V_{1},\dots,V_{n})\bm{\otimes}W
.
Explicitly,
A_{\mathfrak{S}}
comes equipped with natural isomorphisms
\sigma_{\vec{V}}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}(\vec{V}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\varnothing)\tilde{\Rightarrow}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}}(\vec{V},A_{\mathfrak{S}})
for
\vec{V}=(V_{1},\dots,V_{n})\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n}
, between functors
(\mathcal{V}^{\rm op})^{\otimes n}\allowbreak\to{\rm Vect}_{k}
.

Remark 4.9
.

Objects and morphisms of tensor product of categories (e.g.,
\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n}
) are sometimes denoted by tensor product of objects and morphisms (e.g.,
V_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{n}
and
f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{n}
). To avoid confusion with the monoidal structures on the categories (e.g.,
\otimes
on
\mathcal{V}
), we prefer to use commas (e.g.,
(V_{1},\dots,V_{n})
and
(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
).

Remark 4.10
.

A legitimate worry about this extended definition of internal skein algebras is that when one has multiple boundary actions on a same connected component one cannot keep track of where did an object come from. Namely for
V\in\mathcal{V}
and
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
two boundary edges on a same connected component of
\mathfrak{S}
, one has an isomorphism
V\rhd_{1}\varnothing\to V\rhd_{2}\varnothing
which sounds surprising because
(V,1_{\mathcal{V}})
and
(1_{\mathcal{V}},V)
are hardly isomorphic in
\mathcal{V}^{\otimes 2}
. The internal skein algebra actually keeps track of such identifications, and one has an isomorphism
(V,1_{\mathcal{V}})\bm{\otimes}A_{\mathfrak{S}}\to(1_{\mathcal{V}},V)\bm{\otimes}A_{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Note that the above definition makes sense for
n=0
, where we want endomorphisms of the empty set in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
to be described by morphisms
k\to A_{\mathfrak{S}}
in
\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes 0}={\rm Vect}_{k}
. For
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}
one gets
A_{\mathfrak{S}}=\mathring{\mathscr{S}}(\mathfrak{S})
is the usual skein algebra. It is no longer true, however, that all objects of
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
are described as modules over
A_{\mathfrak{S}}
, because the (trivial) action of
\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes 0}
on
\varnothing
is no longer dominant.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a marked surface and
\mathcal{V}
a ribbon category. The reduced skein category
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\rm red}(\mathfrak{S})
is the full subcategory of
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
spanned by objects of the form
\vec{V}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing
, namely in the image of the action of
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)^{\otimes n}
on the empty set.
It is equivalent to
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
if
\mathfrak{S}
has at least one boundary edge per connected component.

Remark 4.12
.

One can still apply Remark
2.46
, slightly modified because for right edges the left adjoint of
-\lhd V
is given by acting by the left dual
-\lhd{}^{*}V
. For
\vec{V}=(V_{1},\dots,V_{n})\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n}
write
\vec{V}^{*}=({V_{i}}^{*}
or
{}^{*}V_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
with right duals for left edges and left duals for right edges. Then, as the notation suggests,
\vec{V}^{*}
is the left dual of
\vec{V}
in
\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n}
for the monoidal structure
\bm{\otimes}
, and
\vec{V}\bm{\triangleright}-
has left adjoint
\vec{V}^{*}\bm{\triangleright}-
. For
\vec{W}\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n}
, one has natural isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}\big(\vec{W}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\vec{V}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing\big)

\displaystyle\quad\simeq\quad\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}\big(\vec{V}^{*}\bm{\triangleright}(\vec{W}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing),\varnothing\big)

\displaystyle\overset{\sigma_{\vec{V}^{*}\bm{\otimes}\vec{W}}}{\to}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}}\big(\vec{V}^{*}\bm{\otimes}\vec{W},A_{\mathfrak{S}}\big)\ \simeq\ \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}}\big(\vec{W},\vec{V}\bm{\otimes}A_{\mathfrak{S}}\big).

Theorem 4.13
.

The free cocompletion of the reduced skein category
{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\rm red}(\mathfrak{S})
is equivalent to the category of right
A_{\mathfrak{S}}
-modules in
\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}
, with monoidal structure
\bm{\otimes}
, by

\displaystyle{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\rm red}(\mathfrak{S})

\displaystyle\ \tilde{\to}\ {\rm mod}_{\mathcal{E}}-A_{\mathfrak{S}},

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\mapsto\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\varnothing,M).

For
M
of the form
\vec{V}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing
, one has
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\varnothing,\vec{V}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing)\simeq\vec{V}\bm{\otimes}A_{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Proof.

We follow the proof of Theorem
2.47
, namely we use
[
2
, Theorem 4.6]
on
\varnothing\in{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\rm red}(\mathfrak{S})
. It is projective by the same arguments and
-\rhd\varnothing\colon\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}\to{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}^{\rm red}(\mathfrak{S})
is dominant by construction so
{\rm act}_{\varnothing}^{R}
is faithful and
\varnothing
is a generator. For the last statement, one has
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\varnothing,\vec{V}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing)\simeq\vec{V}\bm{\otimes}A_{\mathfrak{S}}
by Remark
4.12
.
∎

4.3
Relation for multiple left edges

Let
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}
,
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}\simeq\operatorname{Free}(\mathcal{V})
and
\mathfrak{S}
be a marked surface with all boundary edges labelled left. We show that
A_{\mathfrak{S}}\simeq\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})
as
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}
-comodule-algebras.

Proposition 4.14
.

There is an equivalence of categories
\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}\text{-}{\rm comod}
.

Proof.

The category
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}\text{-}{\rm comod}
is semi-simple with simples tensor products of simples
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodules. It implies that the cocontinuous extension of
\otimes^{n}\colon\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n}\to\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}
-comod to
\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}\to\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}\text{-}{\rm comod}
is an equivalence.
∎

In particular
\operatorname{Free}\big({\rm TL}^{\otimes n}\big)\simeq\operatorname{Free}({\rm TL})^{\boxtimes n}\simeq\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}\text{-}{\rm comod}
.

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a marked surface with all boundary edges labelled left, then
A_{\mathfrak{S}}\simeq\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})
as
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}
-comodule-algebras.

Proof.

We give a natural isomorphism
\operatorname{St}
exhibiting
\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})
as the internal endomorphism object of
\varnothing\in{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
with respect to the
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}\text{-}{\rm comod}
-module structure. For
X\in\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}\text{-}{\rm comod}
, we want
{\rm St}_{X}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm SK}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}(X\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}\text{-}{\rm comod}}(X,\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S}))
. Let
X\in{\rm TL}^{\otimes n}
and
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}(X\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)
represented by a tangle, we set

\operatorname{St}_{X}(\alpha)\colon\begin{cases}X\to\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S}),\\
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{1}}^{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{n}}^{n}\mapsto{}_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{1}\cdots\vec{\varepsilon}_{n}}\alpha,\end{cases}

where
{}_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{1}\cdots\vec{\varepsilon}_{n}}\alpha
is the tangle
\alpha
with endpoints pushed over the boundary edges and states the
\varepsilon_{j}^{i}
over the
i
-th edge and in
j
-th position from top to bottom, as in Section
3
but with more than one edge.
It is an
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}
-comodule morphism because it is an
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodule morphism on each coordinate by the same calculations as in Proposition
3.2
. It is natural in
{\rm TL}^{\otimes n}
because it is a natural in each coordinate by the same calculations as in Proposition
3.3
.

It is an isomorphism by the same arguments as in Theorem
3.4
. Namely, let
W=V_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{n}\in{\rm TL}^{\otimes n}
and
f\colon W\to\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})
, split
W=\oplus_{i}W_{i}
and
f=\oplus f_{i}
with
W_{i}=V_{i,1}\otimes\cdots\otimes V_{i,n}
simple and choose
w_{i}\in W_{i}\smallsetminus\{0\}
. Choose
a_{i}
representing
f_{i}(w_{i})
and denote by
\alpha_{i}
its underlying tangle and
\vec{\varepsilon}_{i,1},\dots,\vec{\varepsilon}_{i,n}
its states. Include
W_{i}\overset{g_{i}}{\hookrightarrow}V^{\otimes n_{i,1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes V^{\otimes n_{i,n}}
by mapping
w_{i}
to
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i,1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i,n}}
, and set
{\rm St}_{W_{i}}^{-1}(f_{i})=\alpha_{i}\circ(g_{i}\bm{\triangleright}{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}(W_{i}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\varnothing)
. Then the inverse of
\operatorname{St}
is given by
\operatorname{St}_{W}^{-1}(f)=\oplus_{i}\operatorname{St}_{W_{i}}^{-1}(f_{i})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}(W\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\varnothing)
and does not depend on the choice of representative.

As in Proposition
3.5
, because every boundary edge of
\mathfrak{S}
is labelled left, the product inherited from the internal endomorphism object structure is still given by
\alpha
with prescribed inputs
v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i,1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i,n}}
times
\beta
with prescribed inputs
v_{\vec{\eta}_{i,1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{\vec{\eta}_{i,n}}
equals
\alpha\circ({\rm Id}\bm{\triangleright}\beta)
with prescribed inputs
(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i,1}}\otimes v_{\vec{\eta}_{i,1}})\otimes\cdots\otimes(v_{\vec{\varepsilon}_{i,n}}\otimes v_{\vec{\eta}_{i,n}})
which is the usual product on
\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})
.
∎

4.4
The half twist on
\bm{\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}}

In the last subsection, we only allowed left
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
-actions. We study here how to change from left to right actions using the half twist in the case
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}
.

Remark 4.16
.

As we saw, a half twist on
\mathcal{V}
is usually not necessary to the general study of internal skein algebras, but it is needed to relate them to stated skein algebras when there are right edges, and to mirror their excision properties.
When one sees a boundary edge at the right instead of the left, it has very different effects on both sides. For stated skein algebras, it does not change the vector space, nor the algebra structure, but switches the right comodule structure to a left one using
\operatorname{rot}_{*}
. For internal skein algebras, it does not change the vector space, one keeps right comodules (
A_{\Sigma}^{R}
is still an object of
\mathcal{E}
) though slightly changed: it is half-twisted, and the algebra structure is opposed. To make both sides agree, one needs to switch the comodule structure of the internal skein algebra while taking the opposite of its algebra structure. This is done very naturally by using
S
. Using the other known comparisons, one gets that the half twist on
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}
should be the difference between switching the comodule structure using
\operatorname{rot}_{*}
and switching it using
S
. This is Proposition
4.26
, but we give a more complete and algebraic definition below.

Definition 4.17

(
[
24
, Section 4.1]
, for categories of comodules)
.

A half-coribbon Hopf algebra is a coribbon Hopf algebra
H
equipped with a half-coribbon functional, i.e., a map
t\colon H\to k
such that:

(1)

t
is invertible by convolution:
\exists\ t^{-1}\colon H\to k
such that
t(a_{(1)})t^{-1}(a_{(2)})=t^{-1}(a_{(1)})t(a_{(2)})=\varepsilon(a)
,

(2)

t
squares to the twist:
t(a_{(1)})t(a_{(2)})=\theta(a)
,

(3)

compatibility with product:
t(a.b)=t(b_{(1)})t(a_{(1)})R(a_{(2)}\otimes b_{(2)})
.

Definition 4.18
.

The half-coribbon functional induces a half twist
{\operatorname{ht}}
on the category
H\text{-}{\rm comod}
by
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\colon V\overset{\Delta}{\to}V\otimes H\overset{{\rm Id}\otimes t}{\longrightarrow}V
. It is an isomorphism of vector space with
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}^{-1}\colon V\overset{\Delta}{\to}V\otimes H\overset{{\rm Id}\otimes t^{-1}}{\longrightarrow}V
. The half-coribbon functional is not supposed to be central though, and this means that
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}
is not an
H
-comodule morphism. There is a unique comodule structure on (the target)
V
that makes it a comodule morphism, namely
\Delta^{\operatorname{ht}}:=({\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\otimes{\rm Id}_{H})\circ\Delta\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}^{-1}
. We denote by
V^{\operatorname{ht}}
the vector space
V
equipped with the coaction
\Delta^{\operatorname{ht}}
. Now,
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\colon V\to V^{\operatorname{ht}}
is an isomorphism of
H
-comodules.

One has a functor
(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}\colon H\text{-}{\rm comod}\to H\text{-}{\rm comod}
which sends an object
V
to the half-twisted
V^{\operatorname{ht}}
and a morphism
f\colon V\to W
to
f^{\operatorname{ht}}={\operatorname{ht}}_{W}\circ f\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}^{-1}\colon V^{\operatorname{ht}}\to W^{\operatorname{ht}}
. It is defined so that
{\operatorname{ht}}\colon{\rm Id}\Rightarrow(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}
is a natural isomorphism.

As maps of vector spaces, one simply has

\displaystyle f^{\operatorname{ht}}

\displaystyle=({\rm Id}_{W}\otimes t)\circ\Delta_{W}\circ f\circ\big({\rm Id}_{V}\otimes t^{-1}\big)\circ\Delta_{V}

\displaystyle=({\rm Id}_{W}\otimes t)\circ\Delta_{W}\circ\big({\rm Id}_{W}\otimes t^{-1}\big)\circ(f\otimes{\rm Id}_{H})\circ\Delta_{V}

\displaystyle=({\rm Id}_{W}\otimes t)\circ\Delta_{W}\circ\big({\rm Id}_{W}\otimes t^{-1}\big)\circ\Delta_{W}\circ f={\operatorname{ht}}_{W}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{W}^{-1}\circ f=f.

In particular
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V^{\operatorname{ht}}}={\operatorname{ht}}_{V}^{\operatorname{ht}}={\operatorname{ht}}_{V}
. The square of
t
is
\theta
so
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V^{\operatorname{ht}}}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}=\theta_{V}
, and
\theta
is central so
(V^{\operatorname{ht}})^{\operatorname{ht}}=V
, and
(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}\circ(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}={\rm Id}
.
Regarding the monoidal structure, let
V
and
W
be two
H
-comodules. Remember that the braiding is defined as

c_{V,W}\colon V\otimes W\overset{R_{24}\circ(\Delta_{V}\otimes\Delta_{W})}{\longrightarrow}V\otimes W\overset{\operatorname{fl}}{\to}W\otimes V.

The third condition gives that
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V\otimes W}={\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\otimes{\operatorname{ht}}_{W}\circ(\operatorname{fl}\circ c_{V,W})
so
\operatorname{fl}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{V\otimes W}={\operatorname{ht}}_{W}\otimes{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\circ c_{V,W}
. In particular,
\operatorname{fl}\colon(V\otimes W)^{\operatorname{ht}}\to W^{\operatorname{ht}}\otimes V^{\operatorname{ht}}
is an
H
-comodule isomorphism.

Definition 4.19
.

For
\mathcal{V}
a ribbon category, let
{\operatorname{ht}}\text{-}{\rm Ribbon}_{\mathcal{V}}
be the full subcategory of
{\rm Ribbon}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
spanned by objects of the form
[n]\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
but now allowing either blackboard or anti-blackboard framing for every point. This subcategory is still ribbon and is stable by the functor
(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}
, and equipped with
{\operatorname{ht}}\colon{\rm Id}\Rightarrow(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}
. It also contains the category
{\rm Ribbon}_{\mathcal{V}}
of blackboard framed points.

Theorem 4.20

(
[
24
, Theorem 4.11]
)
.

Let
H
be a half-coribbon Hopf algebra and
V
a finite-dimensional comodule. There is a unique monoidal functor
{\operatorname{ht}}\text{-}{\rm RT}_{V}\colon{\operatorname{ht}}\text{-}{\rm Ribbon}_{H\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}}\to H\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}
extending
{\rm RT}_{V}
and commuting with both
(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}
and
{\operatorname{ht}}
, so preserving the “half-ribbon structure”.

Remark 4.21
.

This half-twisted Reshetikhin–Turaev functor also gives an equivalence of categories
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)\simeq\mathcal{V}
but this time with much nicer properties regarding the half twist. In the usual Reshetikhin–Turaev functor one only prescribes where to send points with blackboard framing and well-placed on the real line. For framed points not of this form, one has to choose an isomorphism with one of these, like in Remark
2.12
, but these choices are quite arbitrary. Then the half twist sends blackboard framed points to anti-blackboard framed points which are re-identified with blackboard framed points via these arbitrary isomorphisms. With the half-twisted Reshetikhin–Turaev functor one also prescribes where to send anti-blackboard framed points, so one controls closely what happens with the half twist, namely the half twist on
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is mapped to the half twist on
\mathcal{V}
.

Note however that unlike on
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
, the half twist on
\mathcal{V}
is not strictly anti-monoidal (indeed it is the identity on underlying vector spaces) but
(X\otimes Y)^{\operatorname{ht}}\simeq Y^{\operatorname{ht}}\otimes X^{\operatorname{ht}}
is simply given by the flip of tensors. A bit like the
R
-matrix, the half twist gives the difference between the monoidal structure on
H\text{-}{\rm comod}
and the symmetric one on
{\rm Vect}_{k}
. Formally, this error lies in the fact that the inclusion of
\mathcal{V}
in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}\big(\mathbb{R}^{2}\big)
is only monoidal up to natural isomorphism, and this isomorphism, given in Remark
2.14
, maps by the half twist to the flip of tensors.

In the case of
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
, we can define a half-coribbon functional on the generators by
t\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-q^{\frac{5}{2}}\\
q^{\frac{1}{2}}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. This tells in particular how the half twist acts on the standard corepresentation
V
, namely
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}(v_{+})=q^{\frac{1}{2}}v_{-}=C(-)^{-1}v_{-}
and
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}(v_{-})=-q^{\frac{5}{2}}v_{+}=C(+)^{-1}v_{+}
. For states
\eta\in\{\pm\}
, we will write
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}(\eta)=-\eta.C(-\eta)^{-1}
.
Note that
[
24
]
introduce another half-coribbon element corresponding to the matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&q^{\frac{3}{2}}\\
-q^{\frac{3}{2}}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, but our choice is imposed by conventions from stated skein algebras.

To define it on all
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
we prefer a geometric description on
\mathscr{S}(B)
. We would like to give the same definition as for the coribbon functional
\theta
with a half twist instead of a full twist, but in the definition of stated skein algebras one only allows upward-framed boundary points, which would map to downward-framed points after the half twist. Still, we know how to do the “global” half twist on many strands (without twisting the framing), and we only need to add a “local” half twist on each strand, which are implicitly coloured by
V
, on which we know how the half twist acts.

Proposition 4.22
.

The coribbon Hopf algebra
\mathscr{S}(B)
is half-coribbon with half-coribbon functional

t\raisebox{-3.0pt}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{24.0pt}\right.$}\!\!\hbox to73.23pt{\vbox to50.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.43085pt\lower-25.27414pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

By Remark
2.35
,
{\operatorname{ht}}_{\mathscr{S}(B)}(\alpha)=({\rm Id}\otimes t)\circ\Delta(\alpha)
is the stated tangle represented in the middle, and by a left version of Remark
2.35
,
(t\otimes{\rm Id})\circ\Delta(\alpha)
is the stated tangle represented in the right.

Proof.

These two formulations prove that
t
is well defined on
\mathscr{S}(B)
as it respects the boundary relations on the left edge by the first and on the right one by the second. So we begin by proving that these two formulations actually coincide. Let
\beta\in\mathscr{S}(B)
, then

\displaystyle\varepsilon\raisebox{-3.0pt}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{24.0pt}\right.$}\!\!\hbox to121.04pt{\vbox to50.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.43085pt\lower-25.27414pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

where the second equality is only a change of picture representation, not of stated tangles, coming from switching the orientation of the edges, see
[
3
, Section 3.5]
.

The convolution inverse
t^{-1}
of
t
is obtained the same way as the middle term but with the inverse half twist and
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}^{-1}
on states. Indeed by Remark
2.35
,
\big({\rm Id}\otimes t^{-1}\big)\circ\Delta(\alpha)
is
\alpha
with an inverse half twist at the right and
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}^{-1}
on right states. Thus
\big(t\otimes t^{-1}\big)\circ\Delta
is the counit of
\alpha
with an inverse half twist and a half twist at the right, and
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}^{-1}
on right states, namely the counit of
\alpha
. Similarly,
\big(t^{-1}\otimes t\big)\circ\Delta=\varepsilon
.

One directly checks that
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}=\theta_{V}=-q^{3}{\rm Id}_{V}
on the standard corepresentation. Then
({\rm Id}\otimes t)\circ\Delta(\alpha)
is
\alpha
with a half twist at the right and
{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}
on right states, and
(t\otimes t)\circ\Delta(\alpha)
is the counit of
\alpha
with a full twist (without framing twist) at the right and
\theta_{V}
on right states. This is exactly the full twist by separating the unframed full twist and the full twists on framings:

\displaystyle\theta(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\raisebox{-3.0pt}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{24.0pt}\right.$}\!\!\hbox to46.51pt{\vbox to50.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 23.25589pt\lower-25.27414pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{{}{}{}{}}}
{{}{}{}{}}}}{}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.31248pt}{-21.94113pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\displaystyle\overset{\varepsilon\circ m=\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon}{=}t(\beta_{(1)}).t(\alpha_{(1)}).R(\alpha_{(2)}\otimes\beta_{(2)}).

∎

Definition 4.23
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a marked surface and
e
a boundary edge, with orientation induced by the one of
\mathfrak{S}
. The inversion along the edge
e
is the morphism of
k
-vector-spaces
{\rm inv}_{e}\colon\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})\to\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})
given on a stated tangle
\alpha
by ordering the heights according to the orientation of
e
, then switching height order vertically, then taking opposite states and some coefficients, namely:

It is well-defined by
[
7
, Proposition 2.7]
. Note that
{\rm inv}_{e}
is neither an algebra morphism nor a comodule morphism.

Proposition 4.24
.

Let
e_{r}
be the right edge of the bigon and
\alpha\in\mathscr{S}(B)
, then
t(\alpha)=\varepsilon\circ{\rm inv}^{-1}_{e_{r}}(\alpha)
.
In particular, by Remark
2.35
, for
\mathfrak{S}
a marked surface with a right edge
e
, the half twist acts on
\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})
as
{\rm inv}_{e}^{-1}
.

Proof.

∎

Remark 4.25
.

In
[
7
, Section 3.4]
the counit is defined as
\varepsilon=i_{*}\circ{\rm inv}_{e_{r}}
, where
i
is the inclusion of the bigon in the monogon. Surprisingly enough, the half coribbon functional is then
t=i_{*}
and is simpler to write. This suggests that there is a half twist built in the construction of stated skein algebras. We claim this comes from the passage from right to left comodule structure.

If
A
is a right comodule over a Hopf algebra
H
, it is naturally a left comodule with
\Delta^{L}=\operatorname{fl}\circ({\rm Id}_{A}\otimes S)\circ\Delta
, and we will consider these two as the “same” comodule. Indeed, one has an isomorphism of categories
(-)^{L}\colon H\text{-}{\rm comod}\to{\rm comod}\text{-}H
which is the identity on vector spaces, switches the action as above, and is the identity on morphisms. However, when passing from right to left edges – and comodule structures – on stated skein algebras, one uses another way to see a right comodule
A
as a left, namely with
\Delta^{l}=\operatorname{fl}\circ({\rm Id}_{A}\otimes\operatorname{rot}_{*})\circ\Delta
. Again one has
(-)^{l}\colon H\text{-}{\rm comod}\to comod\text{-}H
with the identity on morphisms. So we have two functors
(-)^{L}
and
(-)^{l}
and we claim that the difference between them is precisely a half twist:

Proposition 4.26
.

One has
(-)^{l}=(-)^{L}\circ(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}
and
(-)^{L}=(-)^{l}\circ(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}
. Equivalently the map
{\operatorname{ht}}_{A}^{l}\colon A^{l}\to A^{L}
is an isomorphism of left
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodules.

Proof.

All these functors are the identity on morphisms and only change the comodule structure. The map
{\operatorname{ht}}_{A}^{l}\colon A^{l}\to\big(A^{\operatorname{ht}}\big)^{l}
is just
{\operatorname{ht}}_{A}
as a map of vector spaces and is an isomorphism of vector spaces. The comodule structure on
\big(A^{\operatorname{ht}}\big)^{l}
is the unique so that
{\operatorname{ht}}_{A}^{l}
is a comodule morphism. We show that it is a comodule morphism
A^{l}\to A^{L}
, and hence that
A^{L}=\big(A^{\operatorname{ht}}\big)^{l}
. Let
a\in A
, one compares
\Delta^{L}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{A}(a)=\Delta^{L}(a_{(1)}\otimes t(a_{(2)}))=Sa_{(2)}.t(a_{(3)})\otimes a_{(1)}
and
\big({\rm Id}\otimes{\operatorname{ht}}_{A}\big)\circ\Delta^{l}=({\rm Id}\otimes{\operatorname{ht}}_{A})(\operatorname{rot}_{*}(a_{(2)})\otimes a_{(1)})=\operatorname{rot}_{*}(a_{(3)}).t(a_{(2)})\otimes a_{(1)}
. We show directly that for
\beta\in\mathscr{S}(B)
,
S\beta_{(1)}.t(\beta_{(2)})=\operatorname{rot}_{*}(\beta_{(2)}).t(\beta_{(1)})
:

\displaystyle S\circ({\rm Id}\otimes t)\circ\Delta(\beta)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=S\raisebox{-3.0pt}{$\left(\rule{0.0pt}{24.0pt}\right.$}\!\!\hbox to121.04pt{\vbox to50.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.43085pt\lower-25.27414pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 17.72 0 C 17.72 6.74 12.25 12.21 5.51 12.21 C -1.23 12.21 -6.7 6.74 -6.7 0 C -6.7 -6.74 -1.23 -12.21 5.51 -12.21 C 12.25 -12.21 17.72 -6.74 17.72 0 Z M 5.51 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
