\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\!\!\raisebox{-3.0pt}{$\left.\rule{0.0pt}{24.0pt}\right)$}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\operatorname{rot}_{*}\circ(t\otimes{\rm Id})\circ\Delta(\beta).

∎

Proposition 4.27
.

Given two right
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodules
A
and
B
one has
(A\otimes B)^{{\rm inv}}=HH^{0}\big(A^{L}\otimes B\big)\allowbreak=HH^{0}\big(\big(A^{\operatorname{ht}}\big)^{l}\otimes B\big)
.

Proof.

The first equality is true in any Hopf algebra by a direct computation, and the second is just the above proposition.
∎

4.5
The general relation

We can now express the full correspondence between stated skein algebras and internal skein algebras, with both right and left boundary edges.
On a single edge, by Remark
4.3
one gets
A_{\Sigma}^{R}=A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}=\mathscr{S}(\Sigma)^{\operatorname{ht}}
equipped with the natural isomorphism
(\sigma_{(-)^{\operatorname{ht}}})^{\operatorname{ht}}
, using the half twist in
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}
. More precisely, for
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}(\varnothing\lhd V,\varnothing)
one has

\sigma_{V^{\operatorname{ht}}}(\alpha)^{\operatorname{ht}}={\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}\circ\sigma_{V^{\operatorname{ht}}}(\alpha)\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{V}={\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}\circ\sigma_{V}(\alpha\circ({\operatorname{ht}}_{V}\rhd\varnothing))={\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}\circ\sigma^{R}_{V}(\alpha).

As in Remark
4.7
its algebra structure is

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}\circ m^{R}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}\otimes{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}

\displaystyle={\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}\circ m\circ c_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}\otimes{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}

\displaystyle={\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}^{\operatorname{ht}}}^{-1}\circ m\circ\operatorname{fl}\circ{\operatorname{ht}}_{A_{\Sigma}\otimes A_{\Sigma}}=m^{\operatorname{ht}}\circ\operatorname{fl},

so
m^{\rm op}
as maps of vector spaces.

Note that because
S
is an anti-algebra morphism, the functor
(-)^{L}
is (almost strictly) anti-monoidal (like the half twist, it is the identity on vector spaces but is anti-monoidal on the comodule structure) namely
\operatorname{fl}\colon(V\otimes W)^{L}\to W^{L}\otimes V^{L}
is an isomorphism of left
H
-comodules. Thus a right
H
-comodule algebra
A
induces a left
H
-comodule algebra
A^{L}
with
A^{L}\otimes A^{L}\overset{\operatorname{fl}}{\simeq}(A\otimes A)^{L}\overset{m}{\to}A^{L}
, namely with product
m^{\rm op}
.
When one has multi-edges one can switch the
i
-th right
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodule structure to a left using either
S
or
\operatorname{rot}_{*}
and we denote the associated functors by
(-)^{L_{i}}
and
(-)^{l_{i}}
, one can take opposite product on the
i
-th coordinate which we denote by
m^{{\rm op}_{i}}
, and there are half twists on each coordinates, which we denote by
{\operatorname{ht}}_{i}
.

Theorem 4.28
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}
be a marked surface with
n
boundary edges labelled either as left
(
numbered
1
to
k)
or as right
(
numbered
k+1
to
n)
edges. There is an isomorphism of
\big(\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes k},\allowbreak\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n-k}\big)
-bicomodules algebras
A_{\mathfrak{S}}^{L_{k+1},\dots,L_{n}}\simeq\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})
.

Proof.

To avoid confusion we denote the stated skein algebra of the marked surface
\mathfrak{S}
by
\mathscr{S}^{R}(\mathfrak{S})
when it is seen as a right
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n}
-comodule and by
\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})=\mathscr{S}^{R}(\mathfrak{S})^{l_{k+1},\dots,l_{n}}
when it is seen as an
\big(\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes k},\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n-k}\big)
-bicomodule. We denote by
m
its product, which is the same in both cases.
By Theorem
4.15
,
\mathscr{S}^{R}(\mathfrak{S})
is the internal skein algebra of
\mathfrak{S}
with every edge labelled as left, and then by Remark
4.3
on coordinates
k+1,\dots,n
one may take
A_{\mathfrak{S}}:=\mathscr{S}^{R}(\mathfrak{S})^{{\operatorname{ht}}_{k+1},\dots,{\operatorname{ht}}_{n}}
as algebra object in
\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}
with skew monoidal structure
{\bm{\otimes}}
. The algebra structure on
\mathscr{S}^{R}(\mathfrak{S})^{{\operatorname{ht}}_{k+1},\dots,{\operatorname{ht}}_{n}}
is
m^{{\rm op}_{k+1},\dots,{\rm op}_{n}}
.
Thus by Proposition
4.26
,

A_{\mathfrak{S}}^{L_{k+1},\dots,L_{n}}:=\big(\mathscr{S}^{R}(\mathfrak{S})^{{\operatorname{ht}}_{k+1},\dots,{\operatorname{ht}}_{n}}\big)^{L_{k+1},\dots,L_{n}}=\mathscr{S}^{R}(\mathfrak{S})^{l_{k+1},\dots,l_{n}}=\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})

as
\big(\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes k},\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes n-k}\big)
-bicomodules, and the algebra structure on

\big(\mathscr{S}^{R}(\mathfrak{S})^{{\operatorname{ht}}_{k+1},\dots,{\operatorname{ht}}_{n}}\big)^{L_{k+1},\dots,L_{n}}\qquad\text{is}\qquad(m^{{\rm op}_{k+1},\dots,{\rm op}_{n}})^{{\rm op}_{k+1},\dots,{\rm op}_{n}}=m.

Remark 4.29
.

A nice miracle with stated skein algebras is that the quantum group
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
, which is used to define the tangle invariants used to define stated skein algebras, is re-obtained as the stated skein algebra of the bigon. One can see why this should be true in internal skein algebras. By Definition
4.8
, the internal skein algebra of the bigon is an object
A_{B}\in\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes 2}\text{-}{\rm comod}
together with a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm SK}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}((X,Y)\rhd\varnothing,\varnothing)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}}(X\otimes Y,k)

\displaystyle\ \tilde{\to}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})^{\otimes 2}\text{-}{\rm comod}}(X\otimes Y,A_{B})

for
X,Y\in\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}
. We set
A_{B}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
with usual first right comodule structure
\Delta_{1}=\Delta
and with second comodule strucure its left one switched using
L_{2}^{-1}
namely
\Delta_{2}=\operatorname{fl}\circ\big(S^{-1}\otimes{\rm Id}\big)\circ\Delta
. The demanded isomorphism is given by
f\mapsto\tilde{f}
where

\tilde{f}(x\otimes y)=x_{(2)}.f(x_{(1)}\otimes y)=S(y_{(2)}).f(x\otimes y_{(1)}).

Its inverse is given by
\tilde{f}\mapsto\varepsilon\circ\tilde{f}
.

Remark 4.30
.

Despite this theorem, it is still annoying that in the simplest case one wants to see the boundary at the right for stated skein algebras and at the left for internal skein algebras. This should be solvable by considering the category of left (instead of right)
\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})
-comodules as coefficients, so it is a minor issue.

4.6
Excision properties of multi-edges internal skein algebras

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{1}\hookleftarrow C\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{2}
be a right and a left thick embeddings in two marked surfaces and
\mathfrak{S}
their collar gluing. Namely,
C
embeds as a sequence of
k
right boundary edges
\vec{c}_{1}
of
\partial\mathfrak{S}_{1}
and as
k
left boundary edges
\vec{c}_{2}
of
\partial\mathfrak{S}_{2}
, and
\mathfrak{S}
is the gluing
\mathfrak{S}_{1}\cup_{\vec{c}_{1}=\vec{c}_{2}}\mathfrak{S}_{2}
. We show how to compute
A_{\mathfrak{S}}
from
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}
and
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}
.
The general idea goes as follows. In the case where
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{2}
both have a single boundary edge, so
k=1
, one wants to describe endomorphisms
\alpha
of the empty set in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
. By Corollary
2.19
they are described by a morphism
\alpha_{1}\colon\varnothing\to\varnothing\lhd V
in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S}_{1})
and a morphism
\alpha_{2}\colon V\rhd\varnothing\to\varnothing
in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S}_{2})
, linked by an isomorphism
\iota_{V}\colon\varnothing\lhd V\to V\rhd\varnothing
, which is just a slanted skein crossing over
C
in
{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})
, see the idea of proof of Theorem
2.18
. One can reconstruct
\alpha
as
\alpha=({\rm Id}_{\varnothing},\alpha_{2})\circ\iota_{V}\circ(\alpha_{1},{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})
. The morphisms
\alpha_{1,2}
are well defined up to balancing, namely naturality of
\iota
. Now, by definition of
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}
and
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}
, they are described by some
f_{1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(1_{\mathcal{V}},A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes V)
and
f_{2}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}}(V,A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}})
. Composing them mimicking the reconstruction of
\alpha
gives a morphism
f=({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes f_{2})\circ f_{1}\colon 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}
, namely an invariant inside
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}
. This suggests
A_{\mathfrak{S}}\simeq(A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}})^{{\rm inv}}
, which we will prove below.
Now we need to define what we mean by invariants of a tensor product in any ribbon category
\mathcal{V}
.

Definition 4.31
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a ribbon category,
\mathcal{E}=\operatorname{Free}(\mathcal{V})
and
n\geq 2
. For
1\leq i<j\leq n-1
we denote the tensor product of coordinates
i
and
j
by

\otimes_{i,j}\colon\ \begin{cases}\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n}\to\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n-1},\\
(V_{1},\dots,V_{n})\mapsto(V_{1},\dots,V_{i-1},V_{i}\otimes V_{j},V_{i+1},\dots,V_{j-1},V_{j+1},\dots,V_{n}).\end{cases}

For
\vec{k}_{1}<\vec{k}_{2}
two sequences of
k
distinct indices we denote the tensor product of coordinates
\vec{k}_{1}
with coordinates
\vec{k}_{2}
by
\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}\colon\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n}\to\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n-k}
. It extends to
\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}\colon\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}\to\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n-k}
by cocontinuity.
We denote the unit on
i
-th coordinate by

\eta_{i}\colon\begin{cases}\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n-2}\to\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n-1},\\
\big(V_{1},\dots,\overset{\vee}{V_{i}},\dots,\overset{\vee}{V_{j}},\dots,V_{n}\big)\mapsto\big(V_{1},\dots,1_{\mathcal{V}},\dots,\overset{\vee}{V_{j}},\dots,V_{n}\big)\end{cases}

and the unit on
\vec{k}_{1}
-th coordinates as
\eta_{\vec{k}_{1}}\colon\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n-2k}\to\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n-k}
.

Let
X\in\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n}
, we want to define its
\big(\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}\big)
-invariants
X^{{\rm inv}_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}}\in\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n-2k}
. One only needs to describe morphisms from any
\vec{V}\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n-2k}
to it. We set

X^{{\rm inv}_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}}\big(\vec{V}\big)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n-2k}}\big(\vec{V},X^{{\rm inv}_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}}\big):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n-k}}\big(\eta_{\vec{k}_{1}}(\vec{V}),\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}(X)\big).

For
X\in\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes\vec{k}_{1}}
and
Y\in\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes\vec{k}_{2}}\boxtimes\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{2}}
we write
X\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}Y:=\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}(X,Y)
. Then

\displaystyle(X,Y)^{{\rm inv}_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}}((V_{\vec{n}_{1}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}})):

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}}\big((V_{\vec{n}_{1}},1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}),X\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}Y\big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}}\big((V_{\vec{n}_{1}},1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}})\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}(1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}),X\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}Y\big).

For
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}
we get a notion of invariants for bicomodules
(X_{i},X_{j})\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes 2}
where we first “merge” the two comodule structures (by the product, in the definition of the tensor product) and then take invariants in the usual sense, namely maps
k\to X_{i}\otimes X_{j}
.

Theorem 4.32
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
be a marked surface with
n_{1}+k
boundary edges with a sequence of
k
right boundary edges
\vec{c}_{1}

(
numbered
\vec{k}_{1}=\{n_{1}+1,\dots,n_{1}+k\})
and
\mathfrak{S}_{2}
a marked surface with
n_{2}+k
boundary edges with a sequence of
k
left boundary edges
\vec{c}_{2}

(
numbered
\vec{k}_{2}=\{n_{1}+k+1,\dots,n_{1}+2k\})
. We write
\vec{n}_{1}=\{1,\dots,n_{1}\}
and
\vec{n}_{2}=\{n_{1}+2k+1,\dots,n_{1}+2k+n_{2}\}
the indices of the other edges of
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{2}
. Let
\mathfrak{S}=\mathfrak{S}_{1}\cup_{\vec{c}_{1}=\vec{c}_{2}}\mathfrak{S}_{2}
, then one has an isomorphism
A_{\mathfrak{S}}\simeq(A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}},A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}})^{{\rm inv}_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}}
in
\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}}
.
Note that one has two thick embeddings
\mathfrak{S}_{1}\hookleftarrow C\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{2}
where
C=\sqcup^{k}(0,1)
and
\mathfrak{S}
is their collar gluing.

Proof.

We describe a natural isomorphism between functors
\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{1}+n_{2}}\to{\rm Vect}_{k}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sigma\colon\ \operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}(-\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\varnothing)&\ \tilde{\Rightarrow}\ \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}}}\big({-},(A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}},A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}})^{{\rm inv}_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}}\big)\\
:&=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}}\big(\eta_{\vec{k}_{1}}(-),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}\big).\end{split}

We write
(\sigma_{1})_{(V_{\vec{n}_{1}},V_{\vec{k}_{1}})}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S}_{1})}(V_{\vec{n}_{1}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\varnothing\lhd V_{\vec{k}_{1}})\,\tilde{\Rightarrow}\,\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+k}}((V_{\vec{n}_{1}},1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}}),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}V_{\vec{k}_{1}})
for
V_{\vec{n}_{1}}\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{1}}
and
V_{\vec{k}_{1}}\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}
, obtained from the defining natural isomorphism of
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}
by Remark
4.12
. We write
\sigma_{2}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S}_{2})}(-\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\varnothing)\,\tilde{\Rightarrow}\,\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{2}+k}}(-,A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}})
the defining natural isomorphism of
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}
.

Step 1
(decomposition in
\mathfrak{S}
). Let
\vec{V}=(V_{\vec{n}_{1}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}})\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{1}+n_{2}}
and
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}\big(\vec{V}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\varnothing\big)
a morphism from
V_{\vec{n}_{1}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing
in
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
and
V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing
in
\mathfrak{S}_{2}
to the empty set in
\mathfrak{S}
. By Corollary
2.19
,
\alpha
decomposes into a pair
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
as

\alpha=({\rm Id}_{\varnothing},\alpha_{2})\circ\iota_{\varnothing,V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing}\circ(\alpha_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing})

with
\alpha_{1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S}_{1})}(V_{\vec{n}_{1}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\varnothing\lhd_{\vec{k}_{1}}V_{\vec{k}})
and
\alpha_{2}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S}_{2})}(V_{\vec{k}}\rhd_{\vec{k}_{2}}(V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing),\varnothing)
for some
V_{\vec{k}}\in{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(C\times(0,1))\simeq\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}
, with an implicit sum. This decomposition is unique up to balancing, namely if
\alpha_{2}
can be written
\beta_{2}\circ(\gamma\rhd{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\rhd\varnothing})
, with
\beta_{2}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S}_{2})}(W_{\vec{k}}\rhd(V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing),\varnothing)
and
\gamma\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(C\times(0,1))}(V_{\vec{k}},W_{\vec{k}})
for some
W_{\vec{k}}\in{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(C\times(0,1))
, then:

(\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}\circ(\gamma\rhd{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\rhd\varnothing}))\sim(({\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\lhd\gamma)\circ\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}).

\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}
\bullet
V_{\vec{n}_{1}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing
\bullet
V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing
\bullet
\lhd V_{\vec{k}}
\iota
\bullet
V_{\vec{k}}\rhd

Step 2

(
re-composition in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
)
. The morphism
\alpha_{1}
is described by a morphism

f_{1}=(\sigma_{1})_{(V_{\vec{n}_{1}},V_{\vec{k}})}(\alpha_{1})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+k}}((V_{\vec{n}_{1}},1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}}),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}V_{\vec{k}})and\alpha_{2}

is described by

f_{2}=(\sigma_{2})_{(V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}})}(\alpha_{2})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{2}+k}}((V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}).

These morphisms are well-defined (depend only on
\alpha
) up to balancing, namely if
\alpha_{2}=\beta_{2}\circ(\gamma\rhd{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\rhd\varnothing})
with
\beta_{2}
described by
g_{2}=\sigma_{2}(\beta_{2})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{2}+k}}((W_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}})
, then by naturality of
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
,
f_{2}=g_{2}\circ(\gamma,{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}})
and the above relation becomes
(f_{1},g_{2}\circ(\gamma,{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}}))\sim(({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}\gamma)\circ f_{1},g_{2})
.

f_{1}
f_{2}
\bullet
V_{\vec{n}_{1}}
\bullet
V_{\vec{n}_{2}}
\bullet
\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}V_{\vec{k}}
\bullet
V_{\vec{k}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{2}}
\bullet
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}
\bullet
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}
\bullet
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}
\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}
1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}}

Thus the map

\displaystyle\sigma_{\vec{V}}(\alpha):=({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}f_{2})\circ(f_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}}((V_{\vec{n}_{1}},1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}})

is well defined, because this relation is killed.

Step 3
(naturalilty). Naturality is quite obvious from the picture: one can insert morphisms from below. For
g_{1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}}}(W_{\vec{n}_{1}},V_{\vec{n}_{1}})
and
g_{2}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{2}}}(W_{\vec{n}_{2}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}})
, by naturality of
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
, one has
\sigma_{1}(\alpha_{1}\circ(g_{1}\bm{\triangleright}{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))=f_{1}\circ g_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}(\alpha_{2}\circ(g_{2}\bm{\triangleright}{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))=f_{2}\circ g_{2}
. Now
\alpha\circ((g_{1},g_{2})\bm{\triangleright}{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})
splits in Step 1 as
({\rm Id}_{\varnothing},\alpha_{2})\circ\iota_{\varnothing,V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing}\circ(\alpha_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing})\circ(g_{1}\bm{\triangleright}{\rm Id}_{\varnothing},g_{2}\bm{\triangleright}{\rm Id}_{\varnothing})=({\rm Id}_{\varnothing},\alpha_{2}\circ(g_{2}\bm{\triangleright}{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))\circ\iota_{\varnothing,V_{\vec{k}},W_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing}\circ(\alpha_{1}\circ(g_{1}\bm{\triangleright}{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}),{\rm Id}_{W_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing})
. Thus

\sigma(\alpha\circ((g_{1},g_{2})\bm{\triangleright}{\rm Id}_{\varnothing}))=({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}(f_{2}\circ g_{2}))\circ((f_{1}\circ g_{1}),{\rm Id}_{W_{\vec{n}_{2}}})=\sigma(\alpha)\circ(g_{1},g_{2}).

We now construct an inverse to
\sigma
by the same steps in reverse order.

Step
\bm{2^{-1}}

(
decomposition in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
)
. We want to decompose a morphism

f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}}\big(\eta_{\vec{k}_{1}}\big(\vec{V}\big),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}\big)\qquad\text{as}\quad f=\big({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}f_{2}\big)\circ\big(f_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}}\big)

with
f_{1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+k}}((V_{\vec{n}_{1}},1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}}),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}V_{\vec{k}})
and
f_{2}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{2}+k}}((V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}})
.

This is easy in
\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}
, as all maps split on each coordinates. For
\vec{A}_{1}=(A_{\vec{n}_{1}},A_{\vec{k}_{1}})\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{1}+k}
and
\vec{A}_{2}=(A_{\vec{k}_{2}},A_{\vec{n}_{2}})\in\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{2}+k}
, a morphism
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}}((V_{\vec{n}_{1}},1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}),\allowbreak\vec{A}_{1}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}\vec{A}_{2})
is, up to a linear combination, of the form
(g_{\vec{n}_{1}},g_{\vec{k}_{1}},g_{\vec{n}_{2}})
with
g_{\vec{n}_{1}}\colon V_{\vec{n}_{1}}\to A_{\vec{n}_{1}}
,
g_{\vec{k}_{1}}\colon 1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}}\to A_{\vec{k}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}A_{\vec{k_{2}}}
and
g_{\vec{n}_{2}}\colon V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\to A_{\vec{n}_{2}}
.
Then, set
V_{\vec{k}}=A_{\vec{k}_{2}}
,
f_{1}=g_{\vec{n}_{1}}\otimes g_{\vec{k}_{1}}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{1}+k}}((V_{\vec{n}_{1}},1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}}),\allowbreak\vec{A}_{1}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}V_{\vec{k}})
and
f_{2}={\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{k}}}\otimes g_{\vec{n}_{2}}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{2}+k}}((V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}),\vec{A}_{2})
, one has
f=({\rm Id}_{\vec{A}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}f_{2})\circ(f_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}})
. This decomposition is unique up to balancing, if
f=({\rm Id}_{\vec{A}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}f_{2}^{\prime})\circ(f_{1}^{\prime},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}})
one can split
f_{2}^{\prime}
, which has to coincide with
f_{2}
on
\vec{n}_{2}
coordinates, and is some
\gamma\colon W_{\vec{k}}\to A_{\vec{k}_{2}}
on
\vec{k}_{2}
coordinates (which are now
\vec{k}_{1}
coordinates after the
\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}
). Similarly,
f_{1}^{\prime}
coincides with
f_{1}
on
\vec{n}_{1}
coordinates, and is some
\delta\colon 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to A_{\vec{k}_{1}}\otimes W_{\vec{k}}
on
\vec{k}_{1}
coordinates. On
\vec{k}_{1}
coordinates one has
({\rm Id}_{A_{\vec{k}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}\gamma)\circ\delta=g_{\vec{k}_{1}}
, so the only relation is
((-,({\rm Id}_{A_{\vec{k}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}\gamma)\circ\delta),({\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{k}}},-))\sim((-,\delta),(\gamma,-))
.

Now,
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}
and
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}
are not objects of
\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{1}+k}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{2}+k}
, but are obtained as canonical colimits of such objects,
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}=\operatorname{colim}_{i}\vec{A}_{1,i}
and
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}=\operatorname{colim}_{j}\vec{A}_{2,j}
, so
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}=\operatorname{colim}_{i,j}\vec{A}_{1,i}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}\vec{A}_{2,j}
by cocontinuity.
The object
\eta_{\vec{k}_{1}}\big(\vec{V}\big)=\big(V_{\vec{n}_{1}},1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\big)
is compact projective in
\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}
therefore

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}}\big(\eta_{\vec{k}_{1}}\big(\vec{V}\big),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\operatorname{colim}_{i,j}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}}\big(\eta_{\vec{k}_{1}}\big(\vec{V}\big),\vec{A}_{1,i}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}\vec{A}_{2,j}\big).

So a morphism
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}}\big(\eta_{\vec{k}_{1}}\big(\vec{V}\big),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}\big)
factorises through a single (actually, a linear combination of)
\vec{A}_{1,i}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}\vec{A}_{2,j}
as

f\colon\ \eta_{\vec{k}_{1}}\big(\vec{V}\big)\overset{f_{i,j}}{\to}\vec{A}_{1,i}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}\vec{A}_{2,j}\overset{{\rm can}_{1,i}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}{\rm can}_{2,j}}{\longrightarrow}A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}.

There it splits as
f_{i,j}=\big({\rm Id}_{\vec{A}_{1,i}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}f_{2}\big)\circ(f_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}})
, and

f=({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}({\rm can}_{2,j}\circ f_{2}))\circ(({\rm can}_{1,i}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{k}}})\circ f_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}}).

This
f_{i,j}
is unique up to the relations in the above colimit, namely for
h_{1}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}h_{2}\colon\vec{A}_{1,i}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}\vec{A}_{2,j}\to\vec{A}_{1,i^{\prime}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}\vec{A}_{2,j^{\prime}}
over
A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}
one has
f_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}=(h_{1}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}h_{2})\circ f_{i,j}
and
f={\rm can}_{1,i^{\prime}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}{\rm can}_{2,j^{\prime}}\circ f_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
. Split
h_{2}
as
(h_{\vec{k}_{2}},h_{\vec{n}_{2}})
, then
f
decomposes through
f_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&f=({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}({\rm can}_{2,j^{\prime}}\circ({\rm Id}_{{V_{\vec{k}}}^{\prime}},h_{\vec{n}_{2}})\circ f_{2}))\\
&\hphantom{f=}{}\circ(({\rm can}_{1,i^{\prime}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}{\rm Id}_{{V_{\vec{k}}}^{\prime}})\circ(h_{1}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}h_{\vec{k}_{2}})\circ f_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}}).\end{split}

Set
F_{1}=({\rm can}_{1,i}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{k}}})\circ f_{1}=(({\rm can}_{1,i^{\prime}}\circ h_{1})\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{k}}})\circ f_{1}
and
F_{2}={\rm can}_{2,j^{\prime}}\circ({\rm Id}_{{V_{\vec{k}}}^{\prime}},h_{\vec{n}_{2}})\circ f_{2}={\rm can}_{2,j^{\prime}}\circ({\rm Id}_{{V_{\vec{k}}}^{\prime}},h_{\vec{n}_{2}}\circ g_{\vec{n}_{2}})
. The first decomposition was

(F_{1},{\rm can}_{2,j}\circ f_{2})=(F_{1},{\rm can}_{2,j^{\prime}}\circ(h_{\vec{k}_{2}},h_{\vec{n}_{2}})\circ({\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{k}}},g_{\vec{n}_{2}}))=(F_{1},F_{2}\circ(h_{\vec{k}_{2}},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}}))

and the second is

(({\rm can}_{1,i^{\prime}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}{\rm Id}_{{V_{\vec{k}}}^{\prime}})\circ(h_{1}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}h_{\vec{k}_{2}})\circ f_{1},F_{2})=(({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}h_{\vec{k}_{2}})\circ F_{1},F_{2})

so the only relation is

(F_{1},F_{2}\circ(h_{\vec{k}_{2}},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}}))\sim(({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}h_{\vec{k}_{2}})\circ F_{1},F_{2}).

Step
\bm{1^{-1}}
(re-composition in
\mathfrak{S}
). We decompose
f
using last step as
f=({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}f_{2})\circ(f_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}})
with
f_{1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+k}}((V_{\vec{n}_{1}},1_{\mathcal{V}^{\otimes k}}),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1}}V_{\vec{k}})
and
f_{2}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{2}+k}}((V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}})
. They are described by morphisms
\alpha_{1}:=(\sigma_{1})^{-1}_{(V_{\vec{n}_{1}},V_{\vec{k}})}(f_{1})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S}_{1})}(V_{\vec{n}_{1}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\varnothing\lhd_{\vec{k}_{1}}V_{\vec{k}})
and
\alpha_{2}:=(\sigma_{2})_{(V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}})}^{-1}(f_{2})\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S}_{2})}(V_{\vec{k}}\rhd_{\vec{k}_{2}}(V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing),\varnothing)
. The above relation becomes
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}\circ(\gamma\rhd{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\rhd\varnothing}))\sim(({\rm Id}_{\varnothing}\lhd\gamma)\circ\alpha_{1},\beta_{2})
. So the morphism

\sigma_{\vec{V}}^{-1}(f):=({\rm Id}_{\varnothing},\alpha_{2})\circ\iota_{\varnothing,V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing}\circ(\alpha_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing})

is well defined, because this relation is killed.

Step 4
(isomorphism). One easily checks that
\sigma^{-1}
defined this way is an inverse to
\sigma
. Let
\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}_{{\rm Sk}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathfrak{S})}\big(\vec{V}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing,\varnothing\big)
that decomposes as
\alpha=({\rm Id}_{\varnothing},\alpha_{2})\circ\iota_{\varnothing,V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing}\circ(\alpha_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing})
, then
\sigma_{\vec{V}}(\alpha):=({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}f_{2})\circ(f_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}})
is already decomposed with
\alpha_{1}=(\sigma_{1})^{-1}_{(V_{\vec{n}_{1}},V_{\vec{k}})}(f_{1})
and
\alpha_{2}=(\sigma_{2})_{(V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}})}^{-1}(f_{2})
, so

\sigma_{\vec{V}}^{-1}(\sigma_{\vec{V}}(\alpha)):=({\rm Id}_{\varnothing},\alpha_{2})\circ\iota_{\varnothing,V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing}\circ(\alpha_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing})=\alpha.

Similarly, let
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{E}^{\boxtimes n_{1}+n_{2}+k}}\big(\eta_{\vec{k}_{1}}\big(\vec{V}\big),A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}A_{\mathfrak{S}_{2}}\big)
that decomposes as
f=({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}f_{2})\circ(f_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}})
then
\sigma_{\vec{V}}^{-1}(f):=({\rm Id}_{\varnothing},\alpha_{2})\circ\iota_{\varnothing,V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing}\circ(\alpha_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}\bm{\triangleright}\varnothing})
is already decomposed with
f_{1}=(\sigma_{1})_{(V_{\vec{n}_{1}},V_{\vec{k}})}(\alpha_{1})
and
f_{2}=(\sigma_{2})_{(V_{\vec{k}},V_{\vec{n}_{2}})}(\alpha_{2})
, so

\sigma_{\vec{V}}\big(\sigma_{\vec{V}}^{-1}(f)\big):=({\rm Id}_{A_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}}}\otimes_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k}_{2}}f_{2})\circ(f_{1},{\rm Id}_{V_{\vec{n}_{2}}})=f

and
\sigma^{-1}
is indeed an inverse to
\sigma
.
∎

Remark 4.33
.

When
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}
, with
A_{\mathfrak{S}}\simeq\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})
, one obtains the same excision properties as in Theorem
2.37
. One uses repeatedly Theorem
2.37
on
\mathfrak{S}_{1}\sqcup\mathfrak{S}_{2}
on each couple of boundary edges to glue. This gives
\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S})\simeq HH^{0}_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k_{2}}}(\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S}_{1})\otimes\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S}_{2})):=\{x\in\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S}_{1})\otimes\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S}_{2})\ /\ \forall 1\leq i\leq k,\ \Delta_{n_{1}+i}(x)=\operatorname{fl}\circ\Delta^{l}_{n_{1}+n_{2}+k+i}(x)\}
. By Proposition
4.27
on all couples of edges to glue one gets
HH^{0}_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k_{2}}}((\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S}_{1}),\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S}_{2})))=\big(\mathscr{S}^{R}(\mathfrak{S}_{1})^{{\operatorname{ht}}_{\vec{k}_{1}}},\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{S}_{2})\big)^{{\rm inv}_{\vec{k}_{1},\vec{k_{2}}}}
. By Theorem
4.28

\mathscr{S}^{R}(\mathfrak{S}_{1})^{{\operatorname{ht}}_{\vec{k}_{1}}}
is the internal skein algebra of
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
, and one obtains exactly the formulation of Theorem
4.32
.

Note that we described how to glue two surfaces along many edges at once and Theorem
2.37
describes how to glue only two edges but possibly of the same surface. The two forms of excision are equivalent, in one way by applying it repeatedly as above and in the other way by gluing a bigon to the two edges of the surface that one wants to glue together.

Remark 4.34
.

This remark answers a natural question arising at the sight of the cutting property of stated skein algebras: why is it not a coevaluation one sees on newly created states when one cuts along an ideal arc? Indeed in the definition one uses
\sum_{\vec{\mu}}v_{\vec{\mu}}\otimes v_{\vec{\mu}}
though the coevaluation would give
\mathop{\rm coev}(1)=\sum_{\vec{\mu}}v_{\vec{\mu}}\otimes v_{\vec{\mu}}^{*}\overset{{\rm Id}\otimes\varphi^{-1}}{\longmapsto}\sum_{\vec{\mu}}v_{\vec{\mu}}\otimes v_{-\overleftarrow{\mu}}C(-\vec{\mu})
in particular matching
+
states to
-
states. The answer is that it is indeed given by a coevaluation, but the stated skein algebra of the surface at the right is not the good object: one must take its half-twisted version. Then the half twist re-exchanges
+
signs to
-
signs and kills the coefficients appearing. In particular we see that there has been a choice in the way the splitting morphism of stated skein algebras is defined, and that this choice seems to determine both the half twist and the identification
V\simeq V^{*}
. This is to be put in light with the unicity of stated skein coefficients proved in
[
18
, Section 3.4]
.

Remark 4.35
.

Internal skein algebras are defined for any ribbon category
\mathcal{V}
, and coincide with stated skein algebras when
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{2}}({\rm SL}_{2})\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}
.
Stated skein algebras for
{\rm SL}_{n}
were very recently introduced in
[
19
]
, and one can expect to prove they coincide with internal skein algebras for
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{q^{n}}({\rm SL}_{n})\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin}
for generic
q
with the very same proof. The authors actually showed it for surfaces with a single boundary interval using excision properties with respect to gluing patterns from both theories. The constructions and arguments of this paper work more generally with any semisimple coribbon Hopf algebras
H
, using the equivalence
H\text{-}{\rm comod}\simeq\operatorname{Free}(H\text{-}{\rm comod}^{\rm fin})
, and are actually
[
11
]
’s candidate for the generalisation of stated skein algebras. The results of this section show that this generalisation extends to multiple markings, and that one obtains excision properties immediately.

Internal skein algebras are defined more generally in
[
2
]
for any
E_{2}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}\in Pr
under the name moduli algebras, and the skein-theoretic description holds for
\mathcal{A}=\operatorname{Free}(\mathcal{V})
. As both moduli algebras and stated skein algebras can be defined integrally, or at roots of unity, it would be very interesting to understand how they compare in greater generality. So far, there is no skein-theoretic description of the factorization homology used in the construction of moduli algebras, but it seems credible that with extra work one could rewrite this whole theory in these integral or non-semisimple contexts.
